ParlaMint-DK_20180509130132	Thank you. As I said, it's for the Minister of Justice. How will the Minister ensure that police officers who are trained after the current two-year training receive the necessary training?
ParlaMint-DK_20180509130147	Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20180509130148	Thank you and thank you for the question. I would like to begin by saying that the crime picture is constantly changing in Denmark. That is why it is important that the police's education should keep up with this. It is the case with the new 2-year police training that it is built around three modules, each lasting 8 months. The first and third modules take place on the school bench, and the second module is a practical module in a police circle. There is no doubt that it is important for the police to continue training. It is part of having and maintaining a strong police force equipped to carry out the tasks facing the police. That is why I would like to stress that two-year education must not be left alone and must certainly not be left alone. The training is part of a larger training programme, with a much higher focus than in the past on continuing training and ensuring that all police trained people maintain the necessary level of competence on an ongoing basis. It is important that we ensure systematic and coherence in the continuing training process, which is why the police have launched a newly developed career programme to ensure this. The career programme means that all police officers are placed on established and clear career paths, and this helps to target in-service training according to the function and duties of each police officer, for example. can train as a dog driver, as a motorcycle officer, specialize in investigation, or whatever it might be. A new police training programme has also been launched to ensure mandatory maintenance of key police professional basic competences and high operational readiness. The programme was implemented in the police circles here in February of this year. The content is determined by the National Police on the basis of an ongoing assessment and prioritization of the operational needs of the police. We continuously adjust and implement new actions in the field, so that the police's competences constantly follow the needs of police professional training.

ParlaMint-DK_20180509154958	The debate is open and the first to speak is the Minister for Health. Welcome.
ParlaMint-DK_20180509155029	Thank you. Today we are dealing with a motion for a resolution on the equivalence of language and qualification requirements between doctors trained in the European Union and doctors trained in third countries. B 136 requires the government to ensure that doctors from EU/EEA countries, in addition to Denmark and the Nordic countries, meet the same requirements for language skills and the same tests of qualifications as doctors who are nationals of non-EU countries. According to the proposers of Enhedslisten, the aim of the proposal is to ensure a high level of patient safety. On the whole, I would like to start by stressing that we must, of course, do everything we can to ensure, maintain and extend the high level of patient safety we have in Denmark. An important part of it is of course also that patients should have confidence in being able to understand the healthcare professionals they meet in the Danish healthcare system. It is essential that we, as patients, be comfortable with the staff who meet us when we receive treatment. I therefore fully recognise that foreign doctors must be professionally capable and must also have the linguistic skills necessary to ensure that treatment can be carried out in a fully sound manner. There should be no doubt about that. It is basically the responsibility of the regions as operators and recruiting authorities to ensure that the right linguistic and professional skills are available. This applies both to the recruitment of Danish doctors and to the recruitment of foreign doctors, whether EU doctors or third country doctors. It is still the individual region that is responsible for organising and organising at all times how to ensure the appropriate recruitment and integration of foreign doctors in each region if there is a need for foreign doctors. It is also up to the regions to ensure that foreign doctors have the professional and linguistic qualifications needed to meet the high level of patient safety that we have in Denmark. For languages, competence is equal to other competences that an employee must possess. So, of course, management must not only ensure that a doctor is professionally capable, but also ensure that the doctor has the Danish knowledge necessary to carry out proper patient care. That goes without saying, and there is no doubt about that either. In fact, I also see that the regions are increasingly taking on the task, among other things. We in Parliament have also put pressure on the regions to carry out this task to a greater extent than we have seen in some specific examples. This is precisely because the regions are aware of the importance of good communication between doctors and patients. I see, for example, that there are regions using language consultants, mentoring programmes and other initiatives to ensure the necessary language skills when they need to recruit doctors from abroad. I know, for example, that the Central Jutland Region has taken the initiative to re-establish a national network for international recruitment with representatives from all regions. It is an important step in the right direction and, as such, something that we have called for and supported also by the parliamentary health rapporteurs that we are working more closely together. Nor should we overlook the fact that there are certain regions which have both experienced and also experience problems in securing the linguistic skills of foreign doctors. That is why there is no doubt that this is an important issue raised by B 136, which we have discussed several times in the health group, and which it is also important that we resolve in the best possible way. That is why I would like to stress that we, as a government, would like to do more to safeguard the competences of foreign doctors. That is, of course, what we and the health rapporteurs have talked about between us to ensure that where we see today there is not enough focus on linguistic competences, it is more important. However, the government cannot support a solution which, on the one hand, will limit the recruitment opportunities of the regions and, on the other hand, will run counter to Denmark's EU legal obligations. It is also important to bear in mind that we are actually in a situation where we need qualified and sometimes foreign labour to such an extent, and that the regions are increasingly using international recruitment to complement the other recruitment activities of the regions, because it has simply not been possible to obtain adequate medical coverage based solely on the doctors trained in Denmark. The ability to recruit skilled foreign doctors is important to meet the medical coverage challenges, and therefore it is also crucial that the regions have a high degree of freedom to be able to take into account the conditions peculiar to the individual position or individual employee. The fact is that it is not in all positions in the Danish health service, for example, that you have equal contact with patients and thus have equal need to be able to speak Danish fluently. In psychiatry, the conversation is a diagnostic tool, and communication has a very great and special importance, and there it is absolutely crucial that there are language skills at a very, very high level. Therefore, we can also see that it is especially for some posts that are focused on recruiting foreign health professionals. Conversely, a specialist in clinical biochemistry or clinical immunology will be able to do his or her job in a Danish hospital without necessarily having a direct patient contact to any particular extent. Therefore, the need for a fluent Danish to be proposed by the proposers is not necessary in the same way. There has to be flexibility in the rules in relation to what is needed. If we choose to lay down uniform language requirements for EU doctors in the legislation, we risk limiting the flexibility of the regions, which is also important in ensuring that patients throughout the country have access to qualified doctors at all, and that will ultimately also break into the regions' ability to carry out their task of providing qualified healthcare professionals and medical coverage throughout the country. We do not want that on the part of the government, which is why we do not want to introduce a regulation that is both contrary to our EU legal obligations, but most of all will make it less attractive also for skilled doctors from abroad to apply for authorisation in Denmark. If we look at the proposals of the proposers, it is our assessment that equality of requirements between EU doctors and third country doctors will extend the authorisation process for EU doctors from 2-3 months to 3-4 years. This would mean that, for some people, it would not be possible to secure employment, but that language training would have to take place for up to three or four years. We simply do not think that this is in line with some of the positions that we know need to be taken care of in the Danish health service, and which are also necessary in order to ensure that patients throughout the country receive proper treatment of a high quality professional. I think, in fact, that we can all agree that extending the authorisation processes could have inappropriate consequences for recruitment opportunities and in this way ultimately harm patients, because, as I said, this is also a prerequisite in some parts of the country for us to have adequate medical coverage at all. As I said, there is also the reason why the government cannot support the proposal, that we believe that it would be directly contrary to Denmark's EU legal obligations if we introduced the same requirements for EU doctors as they currently apply to third-country doctors. As the proposers quite rightly describe it in their comments on the proposal, Denmark has committed itself, by virtue of its membership of the EU, to implementing the provisions of the EU Recognition Directive. This also means that Denmark is obliged to recognise the evidence of formal qualifications received by EU doctors if they comply with the minimum training requirements set out in the Directive. Denmark is therefore not in a position, within the framework of the directive, to require medical examinations and evaluation. We can put in place a check on whether an EU doctor has the language skills necessary to be able to act as a doctor in Denmark, and that is, of course, one of the things we have discussed, in other words how we can also focus more on the responsibility of the regions for subsequently dismissing a person if he does not meet the language requirements that have been set. However, as I have said, we do not see that precise language controls can be introduced, to which the authors of the motion for a resolution have drawn attention. Ensuring the professional and linguistic competence of EU doctors is a subject that we have now discussed a number of times in the circle of health rapporteurs. It is also a subject on which we agreed with the medical coverage agreement that we would find a common solution. And the repeated discussions on the subject also confirm that there is no easy solution. It is important that we find a solution that not only creates new problems in relation to recruitment challenges and poorer medical coverage, and that makes the solution proposed in the motion for a resolution unfortunately. Therefore, equal treatment of the requirements between EU doctors and third country doctors is not the right solution. In the view of the government, we should rather introduce an explicit requirement into the legislation that establishes the employer's responsibility for ensuring the right linguistic competences, and that is also something that we very much want to discuss with the parties of the Folketing, and it is in fact also in line with the discussions we have had so far, and also the wishes I have heard from a large number of the parties in the Folketing, in that we stress the need here for health professionals working here in Denmark to be much more able to master the Danish language. In this way, we also retain the degree of flexibility that must be maintained in the regions, also in order to meet the medical coverage challenges, both those that are geographical, but also those that are specially based and that are ultimately about ensuring a high level of patient safety in Denmark and that are about ensuring that we can offer high-quality treatment to citizens throughout the country. So the government cannot support the proposal as it stands, but we share the desire to raise the linguistic skills of the employees who come from abroad and who work in the Danish health sector. And we also experience that we are in a good position with the Parliamentary Health rapporteurs in discussing how we can lift it together.

ParlaMint-DK_20180509161433	When I feel safe as a citizen and patient in the Danish health system, it requires two things: 1) that the doctor or health professional I meet has the right skills and qualifications to provide the best possible treatment for me, and 2) that I understand what the doctor or health professional tells me about what is wrong with me and how I can get well again. I have this security with Danish doctors and health professionals from the Nordic countries, as the education is similar in the Nordic countries. We know this because in Denmark we have a lot of foreign students from Nordic countries in our medicine study. We have a problem now and we will have it in the future with a medical shortage, which is why we are trying to attract doctors from other countries. When this happens, it is important that the Danish Health Authority checks their qualifications and makes demands on their language skills, so that no mistakes or misunderstandings arise between the doctor and the patient. It is important for patients' safety to be the first priority, not the European Union's freedom to provide services. It is also important that the Board for Patient Safety looks with critical glasses at the qualifications of foreign doctors and does not relax in quality because there is a medical shortage in certain areas of Denmark. Unfortunately, there have been a large number of cases of medical shortages due to lack of qualifications from doctors from third countries and EU Member States to such an extent that the entire neurological department of the hospital in Slagelse has had to be shut down. Doctors without proper qualifications have been able to operate on Danish patients without any intervention. This is therefore a fairly wide-ranging problem. The number of foreign doctors, including EU/EEA doctors, in the Danish healthcare system is growing year by year. As can also be seen from the Enhedslisten's motion for a resolution, a large proportion of foreign doctors work in smaller hospitals in the outermost regions of the country. For example: is over one in four doctors in North Jutland from abroad. Denmark must not become a magnet for ill-trained doctors. The proposal of the unitary list that doctors from EU Member States meet with the same requirements for language skills and the same test of qualifications as applicants from third countries is therefore, in principle, right and should not be a problem in relation to EU rules either. The EU directive on the recognition of professional qualifications, in Article 53, opens up the possibility that persons whose professional qualifications recognised by Denmark must have the necessary language skills in order to be able to work as a doctor. The directive thus provides for the possibility of introducing evaluation recruitments, as proposed by the Enhedslisten. The problem with the recruitment of doctors from third countries is that they can continue as a doctor, even if there are problems with professional competence, and it is also important that evaluation recruitment is stopped and no authorisation is given if the trial period is not passed. There is also a lack, as I was able to read, of the creation of a kind of central register. A central register is necessary in order not to repeat the case of Nykøbing Falster Hospital, where foreign doctors were denied in both Aalborg and Thisted, yet were allowed to work as specialists at Nykøbing Falster Hospital. The evaluation recruitments are thus not an infallible system, and in the Danish People's Party we also look critically at that system. Having said that, the starting point is that we in the Danish People's Party support the fact that there will be a test of all foreign doctors, with the remark that it must not be left alone and that of course the regions must continue to be responsible. In addition, a central register would be appropriate to ensure that doctors with a lack of qualifications are stopped before causing injury to patients. In the Danish People's Party, we believe that citizens are better off waiting a little longer for them to go through than to come to a doctor who is very likely to fail because he does not have the right qualifications or can speak the language. Unfortunately, today we have a problem with a great many doctors not speaking properly Danish, even though we from the Danish People's Party had it written into the medical coverage agreement. As stated in the medical coverage agreement: • The interview parties also consider it important that patients experience that they are understood and that they understand messages they receive from health professionals. In order to ensure the proper focus on this, it will be made clear to the regions that it is the responsibility of the regions that the staff of the regions may be involved. Clinics run by the public and regional authorities have the necessary knowledge in the Danish language. The Danish People's Party can therefore support the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20180509161857	It is not often that I agree with the Enhedslisten, and that is not the case today, but I will go so far as to say that Enhedslisten is onto something. That is why it was crucial for us in the Left that the medical coverage agreement, which all parties supported, stressed that patients should understand linguistically the information they receive from the health professionals of the regions, and that they should be understood. I'd like to share a little personal experience with you. I am the mother of three children, my eldest was born abroad, and it was a priority for me to learn the local language so that I could make myself, also in a conversation with doctors, nurses, gynecologists and midwives etc. And everything went well; I understood and understood. A few years later I returned to Denmark, and my daughter was born in a local hospital, and it was a really good experience until the day we were to be released together. For the doctor did not speak Danish, hardly English, so we were discharged without one word from the doctor, but with a thumbs up as a signal that everything was good and that we could go home. Had I now been a cancer patient, or had the situation been that a family member was seriously ill, I probably wouldn't have taken the experience as lightly as I did then, when I chose instead to have a talk with the nurse about how to behave when I had received a cesarean section. No, there is no discussion: the regions must ensure that doctors have the linguistic skills appropriate to the task to be solved. It is clear that if you are to do research, it may not be as necessary for you to speak Danish at the same level as is required by a doctor-patient consultation. There is a lot going on in the regions on the language front, and I really welcome the fact that there are regions offering mentoring programmes to foreign doctors and language consultants who follow the development of the language skills of the individual doctor. I mentioned earlier in my rapporteur's medical coverage agreement and it is absolutely essential. For if, as the Enhedslisten proposes, we equate requirements for language competence and have the same tests for qualifications for doctors from the European Union and the EEA and third countries, then we will be at odds with the EU directive on recognition of professional qualifications, and that will be so difficult for us. For an equivalence, the authorisation process, currently 2 to 3 months for EU and EEA doctors, will be extended by 3 to 4 years. We in Denmark need foreign labour, including health professionals, if we are to counter the challenges of medical coverage. However, we would like the Left to tighten up legislation so that it becomes even clearer that the regions must ensure that the doctor has the necessary linguistic skills, regardless of the function or position to be employed by the doctor. It could also be imagined that we from Christiansborg's side, more than is the case today, provide the regions with tools that can contribute to the learning of foreign doctors. It could, for example, be done. be some kind of language package. The Left cannot support the motion for a resolution, but supports the fact that we are ensuring a clear indication that foreign doctors who are employed in Denmark with a function such as this. involving patient contact, must be able to communicate in an understandable Danish language, so that the doctor and patient have the best conditions for a successful course of treatment, and that the requirements for linguistic skills are also dependent on the task or function that the doctor is required to perform. I welcome the Conservative People's Party, which unfortunately cannot be present in the Chamber today, and say that the resolution cannot be supported here either.

ParlaMint-DK_20180509170511	The bill we are discussing today consists of two parts. When 121 years' work is moved from Copenhagen to Aarhus and 20 new posts are created, there are good opportunities for synergies and close collaboration across the Danish Agency for Patient Safety, which already has an office in Aarhus, and the new Board for Patient Complaints. Close collaboration is also the password in many sports and sports clubs when volunteers, unpaid health professionals contribute to the club during e.g. games, sports events, festivals, or when the doctor shows up for work. Unfortunately, we have seen examples of the fact that the legislation on risk-based supervision that we dealt with here in the Parliamentary Chamber back in 2016 has a number of inherent shortcomings, and we need to change that, and we are doing so now. The purpose of the risk-based supervision is not wrong, because it was absolutely necessary to strengthen the focus on patient safety, but the obligation to register has been shown to include a number of situations and inappropriate situations that should not be regulatory, and it must not be so rigid that it slows down ordinary people to live an active everyday and leisure life, and we legislators must not wear shoes so small that we cannot admit when something is a whirlwind and then act on it. The amendments to the bill here provide for exemption from fees and/or registration for, inter alia: voluntary, unpaid medical work, e.g. at temporary treatment sites, at festivals, national assemblies and scouting camps with up to 1,000 participants, for companies providing voluntary, unpaid, professional treatment alone, and the individual guards, so that it is instead the medical guard who, as an organisation, has to pay a supervisory fee. The scheme is modified so that the obligation to register and the amount of the fee depends on and graduated according to the annual turnover of the place of treatment. The bill ensures that treatment sites with a turnover of 25,000 DKK. annual or less shall not be subject to registration or payment of fees, and the bill shall ensure that processing sites with an annual turnover of between 25,000 and 50,000 DKK. must register and pay half the fee compared to companies that make more than DKK 50,000. I am aware that with the changes there is also a bill to be paid and it is in the order of just under 5 million. kr., and of course we have a task to jointly secure funding, which must be handled when we have the full overview of the final registration and fee level. The Left can support the bill as presented, and we are pleased to contribute to a better balance in Denmark while at the same time ensuring good conditions, among other things. for voluntary Denmark.

And that means that we must now map out the adult tooth care area and we must try to find models so that we have even better dental care than the one we have today. The Danes' dental health is an important priority for us in the Left. This applies to the teeth of both children and young people and adults. We have seen a marked improvement in dental health over the last 25 years, especially among children and young people. This is very positive and such a marked improvement in dental health should also be reflected in the economic framework. I have worked in the dental industry for many years and met many unhappy patients, such as: has fallen out of the call systems and for a number of years has not been to the dentist and now is not only suffering from toothache, but also with pocket pain and a great need for treatment. Because it's expensive to go to a dentist. I have also met many patients who would like to go to a dentist on a regular basis, but who, for social and economic reasons, are unable to do so. And it's both sin and tragic, because poor dental health can go hard on the general health care of malnutrition because you can't chew the food, and increased risk of e.g. blood clots can be a sad companion to poor dental health. That is why I am satisfied when the collective bargaining ended, as they did, that with a broad political agreement we agree to take on several of the difficult and difficult issues. We need to look at how we can raise the level of prevention, ensure better transitions in dentistry, ensure that people living on the edge of our society socially have better opportunities for better dental health, and, last but not least, we also need to look at prices and price structure. It is not an easy task that awaits us. In conclusion, I would like to stress that for us in the Left it is and has been an extremely important priority to ensure that Danish patients are entitled to a subsidy for treatment, so that after 1 June it will not be more expensive to go to a dentist. We are aware that dentists have the opportunity to opt out of the possibility of his or her patients being able to receive dental treatment in future, but again I would say that it has been an important priority for us in the Left that Danish patients remain well informed if their dentist treats without a supplement. We therefore support the fact that it is the dentist's responsibility to inform the patient of the conditions under which the treatment is carried out in respect of grants or the absence of subsidies for eligible benefits. No patients should be surprised by marked price increases for example. dental cleansing or examination. Last but not least, we in the Left hope that both dentists and dental practitioners and other actors in the field will be active and constructive players when we now begin negotiations on the future framework and measures in the field of dental care. I have noted the dentist's dissatisfaction with the financial framework, and I can tell you that my office is facing the square where the dentists have demonstrated this morning, and I have also seen the advertisements in today's newspaper. Dentists are, of course, entitled to mark their dissatisfaction, but I hope that no one will talk in a corner from which it can be difficult to get out again, and I therefore hope that everyone will contribute actively and constructively to the dialogue that we must have with each other. The Left supports the bill as presented.
ParlaMint-DK_20180509173823	Thank you to the rapporteur. We shall continue to speak, and the next rapporteur is Mrs Stine Brix, Enhedslisten. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20180509173829	On 28 February, the Board of Governors of the Regions terminated the current dental agreement. The Dentist Agreement is the agreement between the regions and private dental practitioners. It regulates, for example: the subsidy for dental treatment provided by the public authorities; the special dental complaint system, which is its own complaint system; the rules for compensation; the rules concerning forms of business and ownership; just to mention some of the things. The government has urgently proposed a bill. It is always a problem when we deal with legislative proposals urgently. This means very little time for consultation, and it also means very little time for the Folketing to consider the proposal. However, the haste is due to the fact that without a bill it would not be possible to pay dental treatment subsidies after 1 June from which the termination of the dental contract applies. So if we do not adopt a bill, from 1 June it will be significantly more expensive to go to the dentist. In the vast majority of areas, the bill continues the rules already known from the dental contract today. This is true, for example. in relation to the requirements and compulsory duties of dental practitioners and to the rules applicable to forms of ownership and forms of activity. The draft law also continues the system of professional complaints which we know today, provided that an agreement is reached on it. If it does not, the legislative proposal will ensure that there is another remedy, namely that which we know from the rest of the health sector. On one point, the draft law differs from the current rules, and this is about the financial framework. We know that the financial framework has unfortunately been exceeded in recent years, and the draft law here gives the Minister for Health the authority to ensure that the framework is kept. At the same time, it is absolutely central and a prerequisite for the proposal here that patients' own payments do not increase at the same time. That is also confirmed by the Minister in his reply to Question No 1, which was received by the President-in-Office of the Council. 2 which we have received in committee today, when the Minister writes that it is a paragraph for Bill No. L 235, that patients' own payments in kroner and ear are maintained at the same level as today. This is very important for Enhedslisten, because there is no doubt that it is far too expensive to go to a dentist today. Several of my colleagues have also already told us about the very great inequality we have in dental health. This is also one of the reasons why we think it is really important that work is now being started to look at how dental care in the adult sector should be organised in the future. We have a wish that we have dental care which places great emphasis on prevention, which attaches great importance to promoting dental health and which is also far better at dealing with the inequality that we see today, thus helping to reduce that inequality. That is why we are also looking forward to the forthcoming debates that we will have. I also welcome the fact that many of the parties who have given a hearing have indicated that they would very much like to contribute to that work. We need the people who work with the area every day to contribute the knowledge they have. But here and now we are talking about the legislative proposal which is here and which ensures that a subsidy for adult dental care can continue to be paid after 1 June and which also ensures the economic management of the area. The Enhedslisten can back that bill.

ParlaMint-DK_20180509175323	Thank you for your comments, and also thank you for your cooperation in dealing with the situation that we have faced since the agreement between the Dentist Association and Danske Regions was terminated at the end of February, and in this connection I would very much like to thank the parties of the Danish Parliament and you as health rapporteurs for the willingness to deal urgently with the bill we have before us, because that is the condition for patients to be able to receive dental care subsidies after 1 June of this year. After all, the broad consensus on rethinking the area must also be seen in the light of the fact that today we actually have a number of challenges in adult dental care. Fortunately, the Danes have better and better dental health when they leave child and young tooth care, but at the same time you can ask why the activity in adult tooth care has continued and even increased. In addition, we can unfortunately see that we may lose some, and the good prevention that we achieve in the care of children and young people is being put at risk because we may lose our grip on the citizen when he or she enters adult life. The Danish Competition and Consumer Authority has also pointed out that the organisation of the practice tooth care today can be detrimental to competition and lead to high prices for the Danes. Finally, we also have citizens in Denmark, especially citizens on the edge of society, who simply do not get the necessary dental care today. This is something we need to focus more on. The political agreement therefore agrees that, in the future, we will have to look at proposals for new models of adult dental care which can be dealt with in concrete terms, among other things. These challenges. We're gonna have to, among other things. have an overview of the many different dental care schemes we have in Denmark, and we also have to look at the experiences from other countries where there are quite different approaches to dental care in some places than there are in Denmark for good and for evil, you might say "but it is important that we also have experiences from outside." We cannot in any way achieve this by 1 June of this year, and the draft law we are discussing today must therefore help to guarantee patients' right to dental care until we can reach a broad political agreement, presenting a new proposal for a new model for adult dental care at the beginning of 2019. The starting point of the draft law is therefore not to change the provisions of the agreement. We will continue with the bill as far as possible the framework and conditions of the dental agreement that are in force today and, above all, the bill must ensure that patients' personal payments in crowns and ears do not increase compared to today. This is a central and general principle of the draft law. With the bill, we also ensure that, like today, citizens are free to choose dental practitioners from among the dentists who are part of the scheme and where the regions provide subsidies for dental care. From the entry into force of the Act on 1 June 2018, all dentists who have today acceded to the Agreement may continue to treat patients with regional grants. This would not have been the case if we had not dealt with the termination of the agreement and dealt with this urgent legislative proposal which we are discussing today. We also ensure that patients can continue to complain about the treatment, and we do not change the right to seek compensation if they are injured in the treatment of a dentist. On the other hand, the draft law strengthens the economic governance instruments, by reducing the part of dental fees made up of the public subsidy, and by introducing a refund mechanism which, unlike today, covers 100 per cent. of a framework overrun. In this way, we can also ensure that the costs of adult dental care can in future be kept within the agreed framework of approx. 1.4 billion kr. In this way, we will not be eating into other health areas, as has been the case in recent years.

ParlaMint-DK_20180509175848	The debate is open. First of all, I give the floor to the Minister for Health.
ParlaMint-DK_20180509175906	Thank you. With their resolution, the alternative is to make it legal for people over 18 years of age to grow cannabis in their homes or gardens for private use. In the observations made in the motion for a resolution, the alternative does not conceal the fact that the party wants complete legalisation, which also includes sales, but the alternative does not believe that there is at present a majority in the parliament for full legalisation, and therefore the party confines itself to proposing home-grown cultivation to be legalised. However, the restriction on home cultivation does not change the fact that the resolution is essentially about legalising cannabis as a drug. This will mean that the ban will be lifted, and I can say that the government does not want to lift the ban on cannabis as a drug. It is not because the government as such is cannabis frightened, as we have taken the steps necessary to ensure an experimental system of medical cannabis in Denmark, which started here on 1 January, and we have also made a cultivation scheme in relation to medical cannabis in Denmark. But we believe that these are quite different things. It has been important to me that we have as soon as possible created a good and solid Danish trial scheme and some sensible framework conditions for Danish production of medical cannabis, because I and the government would like Denmark to become a pioneer country in this field. And we do that because conventional medicines, fortunately, have a good effect on most patients, as well as painkillers, but unfortunately, the effect is not as good for everyone. Some patients have been living in terrible pain for years and have found by the use of medical cannabis that it has helped them better than conventional drugs. They experience that life has become worth living again. That is why I think it is important, even in the discussion we have now, because I can listen to the undertone, that some people like to ask if you can't just grow a few plants yourself, and if we can't just put an end to the ban, because then we don't have to have such controlled conditions around the medical cannabis, and then you can do it in another way. And there I think it is important to say: No, you can't. On the other hand, with the medical cannabis trial system, we offer those patients a legal alternative to illegally obtaining cannabis. But it is not enough that cannabis can be obtained legally; quality must also be in order. And that is why I also welcome the great interest in developing medical cannabis within the framework of the development scheme set up together with the experimental scheme, because when the experimental scheme and the development scheme end in due course, we will be a lot of experience richer and also have a good basis for deciding how the framework for, among other things. cultivation must be in the future. So no, the government does not suffer from cannabis scare compared to the medical cannabis, and we have also chosen to introduce a THC limit value so that the possibilities of producing and negotiating cannabis-based products are extended to a limit value which is introduced on 1 July of this year. However, neither the experimental system of medical cannabis nor the introduction of a THC limit value are steps we have taken to lift or limit the ban on cannabis as a drug. This is a ban that the government, as I said, wants to maintain, and there are four reasons for this. First of all, there are harmful effects from using cannabis as a drug. Secondly, the ban contributes to prevention efforts. Thirdly, it is doubtful that legalisation will have any appreciable effect on the organised criminal environments. Fourthly, legalisation would be contrary to Denmark's international obligations. And in the motion for a resolution, the alternative has not found any reason to deal with the damage, which in itself can be a cause for concern. I think there might be good reasons for this, because the damage is serious and how serious the damage is, I would like to give some examples. Each rush carries the risk of anxiety, panic, psychotic reactions, and prolonged use carries an increased risk of mental illness. Of course, psychiatricly vulnerable persons are particularly vulnerable. Young people are also particularly vulnerable, especially those with social problems. The individual rush causes a risk of impairment of learning ability and a constant reduction in intellectual and practical ability is seen in daily use. Deteriorated learning capacity and disability are too many incompatible with education, and the use of cannabis can therefore have far-reaching consequences for the individual. In the motion for a resolution, the alternative has not found itself in a position to deal with prevention efforts either. I think there might be good reason for that too. According to the Danish Health Authority, the ban is assumed to contribute to prevention efforts. This is because the ban reduces the availability of cannabis and the ban sends a signal that cannabis is dangerous and can cause damage. In contrast to the harmful effects and prevention efforts, the motion for a resolution mentions the organised criminal environments. And now, as Minister for Health, I am not going to be wise about crime and the fight against crime, but in the opinion of the Ministry of Justice, it is doubtful whether legalisation will have a noticeable effect on the organised criminal environments. The fourth and last reason I mentioned is Denmark's international obligations. Cannabis is covered by the first of the three UN drug conventions. Substances covered by the Convention may only be held for medical and scientific purposes. A legalization, i.e. the abolition of the ban on cannabis as a drug would, in the view of the government, be contrary to the Convention. It is also the view of the UN body responsible for monitoring compliance by States with the drug conventions that the agency is very clear when it states that the legalisation of cannabis as a drug is contrary to the convention. I also think it is particularly worth noting in relation to the alternative resolution that the UN body has said as recently as March that the cultivation of cannabis as a drug is contravening. So there are several important reasons why the government wants to maintain the ban on cannabis as a drug and therefore cannot support the alternative resolution. In my opinion, however, the most important harm is the use of cannabis as a drug. And it is also precisely because of the harmful effects that cannabis was once included in the United Nations Convention on Drugs and Drug Addiction as well as in the Danish legislation on drugs.

We note what the Minister also said, namely that the comments on this motion for a resolution state that the alternative wishes to fully legalise cannabis quote the end. We do not do that, and when we are going to discuss again a legalisation model for cannabis, which we are going to have to discuss today - yes, it is going to be taken up from time to time - then it will change nothing about the social democratic attitude. We do not want to legalise the cultivation or consumption of cannabis. We think it is important that we consider what signals we send to young people. By legalizing cultivation in this way, we will send out a clear signal that cannabis is safe. Cannabis is not There is a wide range of evidence of adverse effects of cannabis consumption both at short and at prolonged consumption. Have you just once met a young person who has smoked hash and cannabis, who has been destroyed and ruined his life? I've met several of them. I don't know how the alternative responds to such statements. I am well aware that there are many people and the Alternative must be part of them who say that it is all right for you to smoke on weekends and enjoy yourself, but I am a health rapporteur and I would really like to take responsibility for people not getting sick and having their lives ruined. I do not know how many young people I have visited in psychiatric wards or in drug treatment centres who have told me that using cannabis in combination with others was what was devastating to them. According to the Danish Health Authority, the individual's hashrus is associated with an increased risk of anxiety and panic attacks, while prolonged consumption increases the risk of mental illness, chronic obstructive lung disease, impaired attention. The consequences are particularly devastating for young people who will stand to become the clear losers if we send a signal like this. Young people are having problems in completing their education and doing their jobs; they are having the opportunity to realize the life and the desires they have. But we know how many people fall out of education on the basis of an abuse, where hashish is also part of it. We do not support this proposal. We think it's the wrong way to go. We think we should use attention and resources to make young people strong, and we should use resources and effort to help those who have become addicted. So do we, and those are the ones I am, among other things. have met at abuse centers and psychiatric wards. This is not a way to go, and that is why the Socialists do not support this resolution. Thank you.

Users can be reached with targeted prevention and information efforts, and those with high consumption can be reached with abuse treatment and those who otherwise need abuse treatment. We also believe that this would be a more effective proposal in terms of cutting off the criminal gangs than the proposal here. We note that the use of cannabis is widespread in Denmark today. About half of the 18-20-year-olds have tried to smoke weed. I think it would be much better if we could meet them with information, information, dialogue, guidance and opportunity for treatment rather than saying: what you are doing is criminal. Let's make sure they don't go to the criminal gangs when they have to buy. We know it's the same people who sell hard drugs as well. It is very unfortunate and quite dangerous. There are certainly unhappy fates which have an abuse of hashish and which get damage from cannabis. We know that from other products too. We must take care of this, and I think it is a good proposal to spend some of the revenue you will get if you make a state-controlled sale on solving some of the tasks that we can see are needed. We have an abuse treatment which, in my opinion, is far too poor today. The conclusion we have come to long ago is that this criminalisation idea does not work and is not favourable. A great deal has now been said about Colorado today, and I would also like to point to Portugal, from which we have experience, which suggests that the decriminalisation that has been done has clear advantages. There's a lot of faith in this debate. When the rapporteurs talk, it is a lot about what you think will happen. Let's try to go from faith to knowledge and see how this will work in a Danish context. In relation to the proposal that the alternative is making today, as I have said, we are positive, but we also have some concerns about the advantages we think there is in a state-controlled market. I think we should continue to discuss this. How do you ensure that it is actually those over 18 years of age who grow it? How do we make sure you know what you're growing so you don't get hurt? ParlaMint-DK_20180509191334	Thank you. Today we are discussing the alternative resolution in the Folketing that it should be allowed for Danes over 18 years of age to grow cannabis in their homes or gardens. And why do we do that? We do this because the cannabis plant has a wide range of qualities, both medically and as a drug. Despite the ban on cannabis, the drug today is widespread to the smallest small crack in Denmark. You can buy it from bikers and criminals everywhere. According to the National Health Service, 48%. among the 16-24-year-olds tried hash or marijuana, and 11%. has taken the substance within the last year. According to the same board, in a survey called the "Narcotics situation in Denmark 2016 • national data·, approximately 41.5% replied. of young people aged 16-24 who have tried to take cannabis. That's more than 279,000 kids. No one knows how many young people consume cannabis regularly, but if we take both young people and adults in Denmark, it should not be counted for hundreds of thousands. There are probably hundreds of thousands of people in Denmark who consume cannabis, and the figure has been fairly constant over the last 10 years. This shows that the government's prohibition policy does not work. The best way to see whether the policy is working is to see whether the market is under pressure. And you can see if prices are rising, and they aren't. My best bet is that prices have been constant almost over the last 30 years, while the drug has even become stronger. This means, in my opinion, that all the efforts to combat cannabis in Denmark and all the millions you put into it are largely ineffective. A lot of actions are being done at Christiania, which just spreads sales and creates gang violence and shootings all over Copenhagen. We have a ban policy that gives a billion-dollar market to the rockers. And what is even worse: The ban means that it is bikers and criminals who decide what our young people buy when they think they buy cannabis. The government has chosen that it should be the criminal environments that advise our young people and all other buyers of cannabis on the quality of the products. It is bikers and criminals who decide whether there are additives in the cannabis they sell or whether there are substances in which to increase cannabis volume or create addiction. And frankly, is it not precisely the last in the world that we will give it responsibility to hundreds of thousands of Danes? I mean, it's totally irresponsible. We need to get cannabis under control instead of continuing a ban policy that has failed. Cannabis is also a drug that can lead to addiction and problems, not least for young people who have excessive consumption through their youth and schooling. In the alternative, we are in favour of legalisation and the right to home cultivation. We are in favour of the state taking over the billions market and using a good share of the profits to prevent the problems young people face if they have excessive consumption through their youth and schooling. This is absolutely crucial for us. But when this was recently voted down here in the Folketing, we start by proposing that the Danes be allowed to grow a number of plants in their homes or gardens. And I don't understand how anyone can think that it's losing control of the drug. We have lost it already, it all sails, the ban policy does not work, and there is no plan B. Then comes the alternative and says: we must be able to grow it ourselves instead of buying it from criminal environments. And then we are told that it will get things out of control. Well, it's all black. It is therefore far more responsible that people can grow their cannabis plants themselves and be sure that nothing is mixed in, instead of buying cannabis products from criminal environments • products that are not known about the content. We are then constantly hearing the argument that more young people are beginning to use cannabis in countries and states where there are different models of legalisation and decriminalisation. And that myth, it's time to kill. In other words, when you look at consumption in several parts of the world, where there are systems of legalisation and decriminalisation of cannabis, there is no indication that consumption overall is increasing. In some places the opposite is happening. In Colorado, according to the National Survey on Drug Use and Health, which is the state's own research, the number of 12-17-year-olds who consume cannabis has decreased by about a quarter, in the years after legalised cannabis in 2014. In Washington, there has been a statistically significant decrease in consumption among the 12-17-year-olds since legalisation in 2014. In new data from the Washington State Healthy Youth Survey, 230,000 students have been studied to find out if their consumption has increased since legalization. And their consumption is largely unchanged over the last 10 years. I do not exclude the fact that there are individual states, individual countries where decriminalisation for shorter periods led to increased consumption, but all in all it is a myth, and I hope that one will stop spreading that myth or start to document it with studies that are trustworthy, properly credible studies. We must show responsibility and have cannabis under control, and therefore the alternative with this proposal will make it legal for people over 18 years of age to grow cannabis for own consumption at their own home or in their own garden. Such permission to grow cannabis for own consumption will at least mean that Danes over 18 years of age can legally grow cannabis for their own consumption instead of having to buy it in criminal environments; that users of cannabis themselves can produce pure cannabis flower instead of having to buy cannabis in the form of hashish or other products that you cannot be sure of what is in; that the cannabis plant is decriminalized so that it becomes easier to engage with addicts, not least younger addicts, on how to avoid abuse; that the criminal environments lose a significant income from the sale of cannabis. The proposers are prepared to ensure that the details of the legislation are in dialogue with the relevant ministers and parties supporting the proposal. The alternative, however, already points out that in the legislative preparatory work to be undertaken to legalise home cultivation of cannabis, there are a number of points that need to be clarified: how many plants must each person grow or what area must be cultivated? How many of these must at the same time be in flower, including whether it is measured for both indoor and outdoor plants? How many harvested plants do you have to store in your home? How much cannabis can you have in public space? Can homegrown cannabis be given away, and if so, how much? When we have answered those questions and created a Danish model for home cultivation in Denmark, we not only get much more control over the production and quality of cannabis, we also take a large turnover out of the hands of criminal environments in Denmark. I'm not saying that the rockers just disappear, but for every gram of cannabis we grow, the rockers lose sales of one gram. So the right to home cultivation will not replace the illegal sale of cannabis in Denmark, but I am sure that it will make a good start and mean a heavy cut in the sales of rockers. This will certainly mean a great deal of savings for the police and the judiciary, as the sales of rockers will be reduced and the police no longer need to go out and remove two plants from people's balconies and spend a lot of resources prosecuting them. This will certainly lead to a saving that we can use to prevent abuse among young people from day one. For the alternative, it is a dream scenario when we can move money and funds from punishment and meaningless hunting of home growers to prevent the problems caused by excessive cannabis consumption for young people. I could talk about this for a few hours, and I must promise the House that this is not the last time the alternative brings it up, but I have only 10 minutes, so I will finish by saying that the right to home cultivation will also solve the main problems of the medical trial scheme for cannabis, namely that the price has been set so high that sclerosis patients, cancer sufferers, people with psoriatic arthritis can hardly afford to pay their medications if the doctors want to prescribe it at all. If we give people the right to home cultivation, many pain patients and other sick cannabis users can grow the plant themselves, and that is much cheaper than the scheme currently running. So I guess you can't argue that the price is too high without voting for this proposal. Finally, in the alternative, we are still working to legalise and decriminalise cannabis. I would like to thank the Liberal Alliance for supporting this proposal and I would like to thank Enhedslisten for being constructive in finding a Danish model. I would like to thank the Radicals for the same thing. I look forward to meeting with you on 11 June and discussing this at a conference at Christiansborg. SF just votes blankly no. Suit yourself. On the other hand, it is so that today you can withdraw it if you as a municipality invest part of the money you get into parking charges in making proper parking arrangements, that is to say build parking houses and such things, so that people can throw their cars safely and well and without having to drive around for hours to find a parking lot, which is the situation in some places. But strangely enough, that arrangement is also being stopped in this bill. Therefore, there are very few good things that can be said about this bill. You can either take my words for good, or you can ask those who know something about it. I can, of course, ask my fellow party members, but I can also ask them from the governing parties. I have two quotations which I will read out, which have been made in the public debate. One is of the Left's Cecilia Lonning-Skovgaard. She is the mayor of the City of Copenhagen and is a signatory to a debate post, where she notes that the capital is becoming a money machine for members of parliament's dreams. She points out, and I quote: "There has been a ceiling for years on the amount we can keep and therefore additional revenue goes directly into the Treasury. So there's been this ceiling, and that means that what we're getting on this bill is pure spin. That's one quote. The second quote I promised to make will come here. It's sir. Jan E. Jørgensen, who is alderman in the municipality of Frederiksberg for the Left, but who is also, which is no less interesting, a member of the Danish Parliament for the Left. He writes on the social media Twitter on 18 April about this particular bill: 35 million minus, so don't afford new parking garages, really crap. I apologise to the Speaker for the language, but it was a quote from a member of parliament. I think that is something that must make an impression. We Social Democrats believe that municipalities should only charge for parking for traffic considerations in order to ensure the necessary parking space and to regulate congestion. Therefore, in the legislation as it stands today and before this bad proposal, unfortunately, there is no incentive to charge for money in the municipal treasury, because if you withdraw the payment, the money is already going to the state today. That is what we are now going to change and at the same time put an end to the fact that the municipality can obtain deductions to invest in new parking spaces. All in all, this is a really bad proposal, which means that it is becoming more expensive, worse and harder to find a parking space. The opposite is needed, and that is why we cannot vote in favour of the proposal. And I should like to greet the Radical Left and say that neither can they.

ParlaMint-DK_20180509210009	Thank you, President. As has also emerged from the debate, we believe that this is a very problematic proposal that is problematic in its content and problematic in the way it has been and is now being dealt with in the Folketing. And if it is adopted, it will have some, we think, serious consequences for part of the democratic control of the government's work. As has been shown, the proposal concerns an important body of our people's government, namely our State Auditor's Office. After all, they have the central task of monitoring the state's and hence the taxpayers' money on whether they are used, as the Danish Parliament has decided, and whether they are managed in the best possible way. The national auditors can therefore ask the National Audit Office of their own motion to investigate cases where there is a suspicion that the government has managed the citizens' money in an irresponsible or inefficient way. And there are a large number of examples of the State auditors having found reason to criticise the government sometimes a mild criticism and sometimes a very serious criticism which has led to serious recommendations to a minister or which has forced the government to correct. So it is a really important institution which can intervene, but which also has an important preventive role, because it knows at all times that there are economic watchdogs sitting and looking over their shoulders. The proposal we are debating today will weaken that institution. To date, as has been shown, each of the six national auditors appointed by the parties has had the opportunity to ensure that the National Audit Office was asked to carry out an investigation into a case which the person concerned considered necessary to clarify. And the proposal will now shut down that opportunity and demand that at least two state auditors be required to do so. This may sound innocent at first hand, but the problem is that there will certainly be a large number of situations too very often in which five of the accountants come from parties which themselves have a possible share of responsibility in the matter to be investigated because they belong to the conciliation group responsible. And it was Søren Gade making it different. After all, media coverage showed that there was a great deal of disagreement in the College of Auditors. The outgoing President was out saying that it was turkey hunting and that it was political and everything else, and then tried to go out and shun the agreement and say that it was not what had been agreed. So there was And after everything I have heard referred to, it was a question of the Danish People's Party's Accountant putting his will through because he had the opportunity to do so. That is why I am afraid that with the new rules such a case might not have been investigated, and that is deeply problematic, because what is needed here in Denmark is no less democratic control of the government. In other words, we are in a situation where confidence in the political system may not be at the top. We have had a long discussion about the Public Service Act, the cover-up law has been popularly called among the Danes, where people have closed down, and we have a large number of other examples of how we find that power is too close to itself. And that is why it is a completely wrong step to take. Having said that, I also think that the process is very strange and problematic. So, first of all, I think it is worrying that the proposal does not make it very clear that this is a change in the minority's right to request an investigation. That is how it is listed and it does not appear that this is a change in relation to the practice that has existed. It was actually a little by accident that we discovered it when a few days ago this report came in. Secondly, there is the whole haste. So, it's been a week since the Danish Parliament and the public heard about this deterioration in the State Auditor's institution, and now the proposal has to be rushed through the Danish Parliament 4 days later. And it has been allowed to jump into the queue in relation to a large number of other proposals which are waiting to be dealt with, and the normal deadlines that we others have to meet in order to have a resolution tabled so late in the season have not been met. And then you just have to ask yourself, why is this haste? Why should we have such a sudden examination of a proposal that can nevertheless have far-reaching consequences? We have not been able to get an answer to that, even during the debate. And that is why the suspicion is that this is linked to the fact that a new College of Accountants is to be elected, where the Enhedslisten enters for the first time because we have been given the size we have and can occupy one of the seats. That's why the suspicion is that this is an attempt to make sure that new state auditors will use their option in the current rules to investigate cases. It may be that I'm wrong it's not something I can prove but I'm just wondering about the coincidence between the forthcoming election of new state auditors and then the attempt to push this so quickly through the Folketing. So I just hope that all the parties in the Folketing think carefully and remember that the parties that are big today can be small in the future • that those who are majority today may not be in the future. And I hope that it will be voted down or that we will at least postpone the adoption of the proposal for the new parliamentary year so that we can have a thorough and proper debate on it. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20180509211051	Thank you. This good name and reputation must, of course, be safeguarded, but the desire for consensus, which is now the reason why two state auditors are required to require an investigation, we do not think that we should be enshrined in a rigid legislative text, especially if this is to be done at the expense of the current minority protection, where one Member of the State Auditor can demand an investigation. ParlaMint-DK_20180509211401	Thank you. There are no more brief comments, so we thank the rapporteur. We move on to the rapporteur of the Conservative People's Party, and that is Mrs Mette Abildgaard. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20180509211413	Thank you for that, President. And thank you for the fine debate that has taken place so far on this proposal. I do not want to describe all the elements in such a way, and I think that has been done very well by some of the previous rapporteurs. I think I'm gonna jump right into the explosive thing I might as well start myself before I'm asked about it. Somehow, I think it's a little funny about the assumption that this change is coming because Enhedslisten is about to have a state auditor elected, because what is that assumption based on? So, is it such an assumption that if someone comes from the Enhedslisten, then they will just constantly raise cases, that is, that one thing and the other must be constantly investigated, cross-checked? Yes, if it's an independent purpose for Enhedslisten to constantly investigate everything cross-checked without it being justified, then I think it's really fine that there should be two to legitimize something before an investigation is started, but I have no reason to believe that. Well, I have no reason to believe that a State Accountant from the Enhedslisten will set heaven and sea in motion if there is no reason to do so. So I'm not buying quite that it had to be the argument to make this change. In fact, I think it is, in principle, fair that there should be two. And I do that because I don't think you should underestimate what kind of device you start when you start these investigations. Several have been asked: what is the argument for it to be two and not one? And for me, the argument is that we should not underestimate what apparatus we are setting in motion when, as a national auditor, we ask the National Audit Office to start an investigation. It can be enormously time consuming, and it can be enormously expensive. We also have a responsibility for how we use our resources. This may help to make some people, or at least raise suspicions, about some who may end up being acquitted because it has been on too thin a basis, but it is not always that it fills quite as much in the media as when a case is raised in the first place. Of course there must be minority protection, but I think that there is also when it is two out of six; then I think that minority protection is there. I think it can be expected that it will be possible to persuade someone else to see the wisdom of raising a case if you have a good cause. In other words, if you have the good arguments and if you have something that requires a study, then I simply cannot see that you should not be able to convince someone else about it. Then there's the question of why it has to be done formally and written down on paper. Yes, it has been a wish of the State auditors. And as a politician, I must admit that I am very comfortable with the fact that things have been formally written down, because otherwise there will be informal practice, and when it is then broken, you will be standing in uncharted waters, where some will say that practice has been like this, while others will say that it has not been written down anywhere. So this one or two thing isn't the only thing you're adjusting. We can then always discuss whether this has been customary so far. It is also being done in relation to this point of opportunity where there has been a declaration in the past which has been somewhat unofficial, and it is also being made more official, as I read this motion for a resolution, that is to say with the amendment that is being tabled here. From a conservative point of view, it is important for us that we have strong state auditors, but I think we do, after this change. And for me, it is also quite important that it is a united college of state auditors that has come and asked for these changes. Then there has been a discussion about whether this is the right time to do so, i.e. whether it was better to wait until a new college had been put together to change the rules. I do not think so. In my opinion, it is best to change such fundamental things here when you are absent and when there are some new ones who step in because then there are only fewer self-interests at stake, in other words, in relation to your own situation and everything else. So that way, I actually think it makes pretty good sense that the outgoings have been involved in this. It makes sense in my world. Would it have been better if this had happened a long time ago so that we could have had better treatment in this Parliament? Yes, it was; it would have been much better. I was also surprised that it came with short deadlines and that it had to go so fast. I understand that it must be completed before the new College meets, but in the ideal world we had received this attitude from the outgoing state auditors in the past so that we could have dealt with it more fairly in this Chamber. I had found that to be more satisfying. Having said that, I think we can have time to ask any questions that may be asked. I don't think this case is any more complicated either. In those words, the Conservative People's Party supports the resolution.
